 Definition 5.2 (Descent category). The category Desc(ℜ) has pseudocones over ℜ as objects and modifications as
 morphisms. Concretely, an object is a compatible family

                                                      (𝑀 𝛼 , 𝜃 𝛼𝛽 )

 with invertible overlap comparisons satisfying the cocycle law. A morphism is a compatible family of chart
 morphisms.


 5.2 The assembly theorem

 Theorem 5.3 (Finite profiled resolved atlas assembly). Let

                                                   ℜ : Ǎ op → Cat

 be a finite profiled resolved atlas. Assume the chart and overlap categories are small, or locally small in one fixed
 universe, the reversible transitions and comparison cells satisfy the pseudofunctor coherence equations, and every
 construction used below belongs to the verified profile of every participating chart and bridge. Then:

  (i) Desc(ℜ) exists and has the universal property of the finite pseudolimit of ℜ.
 (ii) There is a canonical equivalence
                                               Core Desc(ℜ) ≃ Desc ℜ≃ .
                                                                    

(iii) Every pseudonatural transformation 𝑇 : ℜ ⇒ 𝔖 induces a functor

                                             Desc(𝑇) : Desc(ℜ) → Desc(𝔖).

      If every 𝑇𝑈 is an equivalence, then Desc(𝑇) is an equivalence. A pointwise noninvertible 𝑇 remains directional.
 (iv) If two global morphism expressions use only constructors in the common verified preservation profile and
      their equality is proved in the resolved syntax, then, after every domain-specific hypothesis is verified locally,
      their realizations are equal in Desc(ℜ).
 (v) A coherent pseudonatural invariant family 𝐼 : ℜ ⇒ 𝔙 induces

                                             Desc(𝐼) : Desc(ℜ) → Desc(𝔙).

 (vi) If every local invariant is complete on its normalized chart and the groupoid of local invariant-compatible
      isomorphisms has effective descent, then Desc(𝐼) is complete on the corresponding global normalized category.

 If the semantic pseudonatural transformation is effective for objects and morphisms, then Desc(Sem) identifies
 descent data with the declared global semantic objects.

 Proof. Objects and morphisms of Desc(ℜ) are finite compatible families. Identities and composition are defined
 componentwise. The pseudofunctor coherence equations ensure that componentwise composition remains
 compatible. This explicit construction has the universal property of pseudocones, proving (i).
 A descent morphism is invertible exactly when each component is invertible. The componentwise inverses satisfy
 the overlap equations obtained by inverting the original equations. This proves (ii).

                                                           32
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                      Komorebi P.


A pseudonatural transformation acts on chart objects, overlap comparisons, and chart morphisms componentwise.
Its coherence cells supply the target cocycle law. Pointwise quasi-inverses and their modifications descend, proving
(iii).
For (iv), the equational realization principle transports the proof to each chart because every constructor used by
the proof is preserved. Bridge naturality makes the local equalities compatible on overlaps. Equality in the descent
category is componentwise. The argument creates no missing analytic or arithmetic hypotheses.
Statement (v) is the same pseudonatural-transformation construction applied to the invariant family. For (vi), an
isomorphism between descended invariants gives local invariant matches. Local completeness supplies local object
isomorphisms. The added isomorphism-effectivity hypothesis is exactly what glues those local isomorphisms to a
global one. Without it, a holonomy class, a relative cokernel class, a Brauer class, or a higher coherence defect
may obstruct the gluing.
The final assertion is the definition of semantic effectivity applied to the induced descent functor.              □

Corollary 5.4 (No automatic promotion of directional bridges). A directional bridge contributes a global functor
only when it is pseudonatural with respect to the chosen atlas. It contributes an equivalence only when the
chartwise bridge is an equivalence on the named overlap. No quotient, completion, or compression becomes
reversible merely because it appears in the same diagram.
Corollary 5.5 (Global theorem transport). A theorem proved from packet reconstruction, refinement, residual
calculus, or another preserved constructor descends globally only when every chart and bridge used by the proof
carries that constructor in its verified preservation profile.
Remark 5.6 (Exact status). Theorem 5.3 is a proved conditional theorem. It does not prove that the full proposed
Komorebi atlas satisfies the hypotheses. The remaining work is the verification of the actual profiles, overlap
categories, naturality cells, higher coherence, and effectivity.


6 Verified finite Mahler atlas models

Two exact finite models already realize substantial pieces of the assembly theorem. They remain inside the
affine-Mahler and packet-analysis setting, but they verify nontrivial coherence and effectivity mechanisms.


6.1 Affine-Mahler chart groupoid
                                                                         (𝑑)
Fix 𝑑 ≥ 0. Over characteristic zero, the affine Mahler coefficient maps 𝐴𝑚,𝑠 are invertible and satisfy
                                                (𝑑) (𝑑)     (𝑑)
                                               𝐴𝑛,𝑡 𝐴𝑚,𝑠 = 𝐴𝑚𝑛,𝑚𝑡+𝑠 .

For coprime 𝑚, 𝑛, the two paths around every declared Chinese-remainder diamond are equal [5]. Hence every
finite subdiagram generated by these charts is a coherent finite chart groupoid with trivial holonomy on those
diamonds.
Proposition 6.1 (Rational affine-Mahler chart groupoid). Every finite characteristic-zero diagram generated by the
invertible affine Mahler charts and the declared CRT comparison cells defines a strict finite groupoid atlas. Its path
transport depends only on the represented affine substitution, and every declared CRT cycle has identity holonomy.

7 The normalized C-finite three-chart atlas

This section completes the first cross-presentation model requested by the Work Package 0 dependency graph. It
does not combine unrelated broad strata, but it does prove a genuine three-chart effective descent theorem with
nontrivial morphisms on the state side.

Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                          Komorebi P.


7.1 Persistent C-finite semantics

Fix a field 𝐾. A nonzero C-finite sequence 𝑎 = (𝑎 𝑛 ) 𝑛≥0 is persistent when its minimal characteristic polynomial
has nonzero constant term. Equivalently, its active characteristic roots are nonzero, or its minimal state transition is
invertible [7].
        pers
Let Seq𝐾 be the discrete groupoid of persistent C-finite sequences over 𝐾.

                  𝐾 is a triple

The semantic functor sends a state to its coefficient sequence.
The state-to-OGF bridge is the resolvent

                                               𝐴(𝑧) = 𝜆(𝐼 − 𝑧𝐶) −1 𝑣.

The reduced denominator has degree equal to the minimal state dimension, which equals the Hankel rank.
Conversely, a reduced rational OGF has a companion realization. Reducedness makes the companion realization
reachable, while the companion output is observable. Any two minimal realizations of the same sequence are
uniquely similar through their common shift action on the Hankel column space [7].


7.5 Effective descent across the three charts

Theorem 7.1 (Normalized C-finite three-chart atlas). For every field 𝐾, the semantic functors fit into equivalences
of groupoids
                                                                        pers
                                   Recmin         red        min
                                       𝐾 ≃ Rat𝐾 ≃ State𝐾 ≃ Seq𝐾 .

The recurrence-rational bridge is a strict inverse pair. The rational-state bridge is an equivalence with companion
quasi-inverse. The comparison isomorphism on the state-side composite is the unique similarity between minimal
realizations. Consequently:

 (i) the three charts and their pairwise overlaps form a coherent finite profiled atlas;
(ii) the triple-overlap comparison satisfies the cocycle law by uniqueness of minimal similarity;
(iii) descent for objects and morphisms is effective;
                                                pers
(iv) the descent category is equivalent to Seq𝐾 .

Proof. The equivalence between normalized minimal recurrences and reduced rational ordinary generating
functions follows from the explicit reciprocal-denominator formula and clearing of the denominator. These
constructions are mutually inverse.
A minimal state gives a rational resolvent. Conversely, a reduced rational series gives a companion state. Minimal
linear dimension equals Hankel rank, and the reduced denominator degree has the same value. Hence the companion
state is minimal. Any two minimal states representing the same sequence identify with the forward shift on the
Hankel column space and are therefore similar. The similarity is unique because a reachable state is spanned by
𝐶 𝑛 𝑣, so an intertwiner fixing the input vector is determined on a spanning set.
Choose the recurrence-to-state bridge to be the companion realization obtained through the rational chart. Then one
triangular composite is strict. On the state side, the composite through the rational and recurrence charts returns the
companion realization of the same sequence. The unique minimal similarity supplies the comparison isomorphism.
Any two composites around a triple overlap are comparison isomorphisms between the same minimal realizations,
so uniqueness forces the cocycle law.
Each semantic functor is an equivalence to the same discrete groupoid of persistent sequences. Compatible local
objects and morphisms therefore descend uniquely up to the stated similarity, proving effectivity and the final
equivalence.                                                                                                 □

7.6 Descent of the minimality and torsion-Fourier invariants

For a persistent C-finite sequence, the minimal dimension

                                         𝜌(𝑎) = linrank(𝑎) = rank 𝐻 (𝑎)

is simultaneously the minimal recurrence order, the reduced denominator degree, and the dimension of a minimal
reachable-observable state.
Over characteristic zero, write the unique active expansion over an algebraic closure as
                                                     ∑︁
                                                𝑎𝑛 =     𝑃𝜆 (𝑛)𝜆 𝑛 .
                                                      𝜆∈Λ

For each sampling modulus 𝑁 and address 𝑗, the spectral torsion-Fourier theorem computes the exact minimal
recurrence, reduced denominator, Hankel rank, state dimension, rank-drop mechanism, and finite periodic packet
signature of 𝑎 𝑁 𝑚+ 𝑗 [7].

Corollary 7.2 (Cross-presentation torsion-Fourier descent). The minimal dimension and the complete arithmetic-
subsequence packet-complexity profile are independent of whether the input is supplied as a normalized minimal
recurrence, a reduced rational OGF, or a minimal state realization. The exact compiler outputs agree under every
bridge in theorem 7.1.

Proof. All three charts represent the same sequence. The compiler theorem accepts any of the three presentation
types and computes quantities determined by the active modal decomposition or, equivalently, by exact reachable-
observable reduction of the jump state. The bridge functors preserve the coefficient sequence, so the outputs
agree.                                                                                                        □

The phrase “complete invariant” here has a declared target. The torsion-Fourier signature is complete for the
minimal recurrence and complexity profile of every arithmetic subsequence. It is not asserted to classify arbitrary
sequence presentations or all sequence phenomena.

8 A first heterogeneous effective-descent atlas

The previous sections separated the formal assembly theorem from the nonformal work of declaring and verifying
an actual atlas. This section discharges that burden for a deliberately restricted but genuinely heterogeneous
example. The charts do not merely use different coordinates inside one linear category. They belong to three of the
principal strata:

             finite linear and C-finite      digital and automatic                functional-Mahler and Cartier.

The common semantic objects are unit purely periodic sequences over a prime field. The restriction is strong
enough to make every bridge explicit, every normalized fiber rigid, every nonempty triple overlap coherent, and
descent effective for both objects and morphisms. At the same time it is broad enough to contain repeated-root
recurrences in characteristic 𝑝, nontrivial minimal automata, rational functional-Mahler masks, and redundant
Cartier compiler presentations.

It is regular at the origin exactly when 𝐹𝑎 (0) ≠ 0. The normalization 𝑎 0 = 1 removes scalar ambiguity and gives
𝑅 𝑎 (0) = 1. Purely periodic sequences with 𝑎 0 = 0 remain valid C-finite and automatic objects, but they need not
lie in this regular unit-mask overlap. They are not discarded; they lie outside the selected heterogeneous atlas.

Proposition 8.2 (Finite-field persistence equals pure periodicity). For a sequence over F 𝑝 , the following are
equivalent.


                                                              39
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                             Komorebi P.


 (i) The sequence is C-finite and its minimal recurrence has nonzero constant term.
(ii) The sequence has a finite-dimensional state realization

                                                              𝑎 𝑛 = 𝜆𝐶 𝑛 𝑣

      with 𝐶 ∈ 𝐺 𝐿 𝑑 (F 𝑝 ).
(iii) The sequence is purely periodic from 𝑛 = 0.
If 𝑚 𝑎 (𝑥) is the monic minimal recurrence polynomial and 𝑇 = 𝑇 (𝑎), then

                                𝑚 𝑎 (𝑥) | 𝑥 𝑇 − 1,       𝑇 = min{𝑁 ≥ 1 : 𝑚 𝑎 (𝑥) | 𝑥 𝑁 − 1}.                              (2)

Proof. A recurrence with nonzero constant term has an invertible companion matrix, giving (i) ⇒ (ii). The orbit
of 𝑣 under an invertible map of the finite set F𝑑𝑝 is a cycle containing its initial state, so (ii) ⇒ (iii). A sequence
of period 𝑇 is annihilated by 𝐸 𝑇 − 𝐼, where 𝐸 is the forward shift, giving (iii) ⇒ (i). The minimal annihilator
divides every annihilator, hence 𝑚 𝑎 | 𝑥 𝑇 − 1. Conversely, if 𝑚 𝑎 | 𝑥 𝑁 − 1, then 𝐸 𝑁 − 𝐼 annihilates the sequence.
Minimality of 𝑇 proves Equation (2).                                                                                  □

Let
                                                                     𝑃 𝑎 (𝑧)
                                                        𝐹𝑎 (𝑧) =
                                                                     𝑄 𝑎 (𝑧)
be reduced with
                                                     𝑃 𝑎 (0) = 𝑄 𝑎 (0) = 1.
If 𝑑 = deg 𝑚 𝑎 , then
                                                     𝑄 𝑎 (𝑧) = 𝑧 𝑑 𝑚 𝑎 (𝑧 −1 ),
with the evident coefficient normalization. Taking reciprocal polynomials in Equation (2) gives

                                𝑄 𝑎 (𝑧) | 1 − 𝑧𝑇 ,       𝑇 = min{𝑁 ≥ 1 : 𝑄 𝑎 (𝑧) | 1 − 𝑧 𝑁 }.                             (3)

Thus pure periodicity is visible both in the linear annihilator and in the reduced rational denominator.


8.2 Four normalized charts

The reversible core of the selected atlas has four charts. Each chart carries its own native objects and morphisms,
but every normalized fiber over a fixed sequence will be shown to be contractible.


8.2.1 The minimal recurrence chart
                   ×,per
An object of Rec 𝑝         is the normalized minimal recurrence polynomial

                                      𝑚(𝑥) = 𝑥 𝑑 + 𝑐 1 𝑥 𝑑−1 + · · · + 𝑐 𝑑 ,      𝑐 𝑑 ≠ 0,

together with the initial vector (𝑎 0 , . . . , 𝑎 𝑑−1 ), subject to 𝑎 0 = 1 and the requirement that the generated sequence is
purely periodic. Minimality means that no monic recurrence of lower degree annihilates the sequence. Morphisms
are recurrence-presentation isomorphisms preserving the coefficient sequence. Because the minimal annihilator
and initial data are unique, every endomorphism is the identity.




                                                                40
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                                      Komorebi P.


8.2.2 The reduced rational chart
                     ×,per
An object of Rat 𝑝           is a reduced fraction

                                                   𝑃(𝑧)
                                         𝐹 (𝑧) =        ∈ F 𝑝 (𝑧),          𝑃(0) = 𝑄(0) = 1,
                                                   𝑄(𝑧)

whose coefficient sequence is purely periodic. Equivalently, 𝑄 | 1 − 𝑧𝑇 for some 𝑇 and the reduced coefficient
sequence has no transient prefix. Morphisms preserve the rational function. Reducedness and the constant-term
normalization make this chart rigid.
The recurrence-rational bridge is the strict inverse pair already used in the C-finite atlas: multiply the generating
series by the reciprocal recurrence denominator, or recover the recurrence by clearing the reduced denominator.
On the present finite-field subcategory, the additional condition 𝑄 | 1 − 𝑧𝑇 records pure periodicity.


8.2.3 The canonical digital chart

For 0 ≤ 𝑑 < 𝑝, define the Cartier section of a sequence by

The 𝑝-kernel is                                  

We use least-significant-digit-first words. Every finite digit word is allowed. Appending high zero digits may
change the internal state but never changes the represented coefficient, because the output of a state 𝑏 is 𝑏 0 and

Proof. After ℓ digits, the resulting subsequence is 𝑛 ↦→ 𝑎 𝑝ℓ 𝑛+𝑟 , which depends only on ( 𝑝 ℓ mod 𝑇, 𝑟 mod 𝑇).

                                   Λ𝑑 𝑏 𝑢,𝑟 (𝑛) = 𝑏 𝑢,𝑟 ( 𝑝𝑛 + 𝑑) = 𝑤 ( 𝑝𝑢)𝑛+(𝑟+𝑢𝑑) ,
proving Equation (6). The pair set is finite, so the kernel is finite. Every kernel state is reachable by construction.
If two kernel sequences differ, they differ at some 𝑛; reading the base-𝑝 digits of that 𝑛 distinguishes the states by
their outputs. Hence the kernel automaton is minimal. Any other reachable minimal automaton producing the
same sequence is uniquely isomorphic to it by the usual behavioral quotient.                                         □

The pair model gives a useful finite bound. Write

                                          𝑇 = 𝑝 𝜈 𝑀,           gcd(𝑀, 𝑝) = 1,

Lemma 8.4 (Exact multiplier-orbit size). The set 𝑈 𝑝 (𝑇) has cardinality

                                              |𝑈 𝑝 (𝑇)| = 𝜈 + ord 𝑀 ( 𝑝).                                          (7)

                                            |K 𝑝 (𝑎)| ≤ 𝑇 𝜈 + ord 𝑀 ( 𝑝) .                                         (8)

Proof. For 0 ≤ 𝑒 < 𝜈, the residues 𝑝 𝑒 mod 𝑝 𝜈 have distinct 𝑝-adic valuations. For 𝑒 ≥ 𝜈, the residue is zero
modulo 𝑝 𝜈 , while modulo 𝑀 it runs through one multiplicative cycle of length ord 𝑀 ( 𝑝). The early and eventual
residues cannot coincide. This proves Equation (7). The kernel is a quotient of the pair set 𝑈 𝑝 (𝑇) × Z/𝑇Z, giving
Equation (8).                                                                                                     □

lies in Per 𝑝 . Morphisms are automaton isomorphisms preserving the start state, transitions, and outputs.
Reachability forces every automorphism to be the identity.

8.3.1 The rational-to-digital construction

For a periodic word 𝑤 of length 𝑇, construct the finite pair automaton from Equation (6), then quotient by equality
of the induced subsequences. This gives the canonical minimal DFAO directly. Conversely, a minimal DFAO
in the selected chart is promised to produce a purely periodic sequence. Its minimal period can be recovered
exactly by enumerating 𝑁 = 1, 2, . . ., constructing the finite-state transducer for addition by 𝑁, and testing
equivalence between the original output and its 𝑁-shift. Equivalence of finite deterministic output automata is
a finite product-graph calculation. The promise of pure periodicity guarantees termination. Once 𝑇 is known,
Equation (1) gives the rational series, which is then reduced.
This inverse is effective but not claimed to be complexity optimal. The point is effectivity, not a sharp automata
algorithm.

Proposition 8.7 (Compiler reachability lands in the canonical digital chart). For a mask in Mask 𝑝 , the reachable
part of the fixed-denominator Cartier compiler is uniquely isomorphic to the canonical 𝑝-kernel automaton of
theorem 8.3. In particular, the subsequent quotient by equality of future output behavior is trivial.

Proof. The fixed-denominator intertwining map sends the numerator reached by a digit word 𝑤 to the Cartier
section Λ𝑤 𝐹. It is injective, as proved in the more general theorems 9.4 and 9.7. Hence distinct reachable numerator
states represent distinct section series and are distinguished by some future coefficient. The reachable compiler
state set is therefore exactly the canonical 𝑝-kernel state set, with matching transitions and outputs.            □


8.4 Triple-overlap and quadruple coherence

There are four nonempty triple overlaps:

             (Rec, Rat, Aut),      (Rec, Rat, Mask),             (Rec, Aut, Mask),         (Rat, Aut, Mask).

All four charts meet on the same unit periodic semantic objects.

Lemma 8.8 (Rigidity of normalized fibers). Fix 𝑎 ∈ Per 𝑝                           . In each of the four normalized charts, the groupoid
of presentations of 𝑎 is contractible.

Proof. The minimal recurrence with its initial data, the reduced rational function, and the reduced mask are unique,
so their fibers are terminal groupoids. A normalized digital presentation is a reachable minimal automaton. Any
automorphism fixes the start state and commutes with every transition. Reachability then forces it to fix every state.
Hence its automorphism group is trivial, and any two minimal presentations are joined by the unique behavioral
isomorphism.                                                                                                        □

Theorem 8.9 (Coherence of the periodic heterogeneous atlas). Choose every pairwise bridge by the explicit
constructions above and choose every comparison 2-cell to be the unique normalized isomorphism over the common
sequence. Then:
 (i) every one of the four triple-overlap comparison diagrams commutes;
(ii) the two composites of comparison cells on the four-chart overlap agree;
(iii) the quadruple-overlap coherence defect Ω is the identity.

Proof. Every comparison in question is an isomorphism between normalized presentations of one fixed semantic
sequence. By theorem 8.8, there is exactly one such isomorphism. Any two composites with the same source and
target must therefore agree. This proves all triple laws and the quadruple law simultaneously.             □

Figure 13: The coherence tetrahedron. Contractibility of every normalized semantic fiber makes each comparison

Theorem 8.10 (First heterogeneous effective-descent theorem). The semantic pseudonatural transformation
induces an equivalence
                                                 per      ×,per
                                         Desc(ℜ 𝑝 ) ≃ Per 𝑝 .                                      (15)
In particular, descent is effective for both objects and morphisms.
                            ×,per
Proof. Given 𝑎 ∈ Per 𝑝 , form its minimal recurrence, reduced rational OGF, canonical minimal 𝑝-kernel
automaton, and reduced rational mask. The explicit bridges supply a descent object. This defines a functor
                                                                ×,per                   per
                                                     𝑁 : Per 𝑝          −→ Desc(ℜ 𝑝 ).

Conversely, any descent object has local presentations whose semantic images agree on every overlap. Since the
semantic target is discrete, they determine one sequence 𝑎. Normalization and theorem 8.8 identify the local
presentations with those constructed by 𝑁 (𝑎). Thus 𝑁 is essentially surjective and fully faithful.
For morphisms, a compatible local family lies over one equality of semantic sequences. Each normalized fiber has
at most one morphism, so the local family glues uniquely. Identities, composition, equality, and invertibility are
therefore detected componentwise and descend.                                                                   □
                                                                                                        per
Corollary 8.11 (A verified instance of the assembly theorem). The atlas ℜ 𝑝 satisfies realization, semantic
adequacy, typed overlaps, higher coherence, effective descent, and preservation for the finite algebraic constructions
used in this section. Therefore theorem 5.3 applies without a remaining categorical hypothesis on this restricted
domain.

The conclusion is intentionally local to the selected atlas. It does not imply effective descent for arbitrary automatic
sequences, unrestricted rational masks, general local analytic objects, or the full eight-stratum registry.

Theorem 8.12 (Invariant descent and global completeness on the periodic atlas). The native invariants in the
recurrence, rational, digital, and mask charts admit coherent comparison maps to J𝑝 . These maps descend to
                                                                       per         per
                                                        J𝑝 : Desc(ℜ 𝑝 ) −→ Inv 𝑝 .

The descended invariant is complete on the declared semantic category.

Proof. Every component of J𝑝 (𝑎) is reconstructed from the coefficient sequence by the explicit normalization
procedures above. Hence every pairwise bridge preserves the package and the comparison cells are identities after
semantic identification. Triple coherence follows from theorem 8.9. If J𝑝 (𝑎) = J𝑝 (𝑏), then the primitive period
words agree, so 𝑎 = 𝑏. Under Equation (15), equality of semantic objects is exactly isomorphism in the descent
category.                                                                                                       □

The completeness claim has a narrow classification target: unit purely periodic sequences over F 𝑝 . It is not a
complete invariant for all C-finite, all automatic, or all rational functional-Mahler sequences.

  Block 03 result. The manuscript now contains a fully declared finite atlas spanning three genuinely different
  strata: minimal finite-linear recurrences, minimal digital automata, and rational functional-Mahler masks.
  Every reversible bridge has a complete passport. The raw Cartier compiler is retained as a directional
  constructor with an exact loss statement. All four triple overlaps and the quadruple coherence equation are
  proved through rigidity of normalized fibers. Objects and morphisms descend effectively, and the compatible
  periodic invariant package is globally complete on the declared unit-periodic semantic category. This is the
  first theorem in the manuscript that satisfies the finite heterogeneous completion criterion rather than only the
  one-stratum cross-presentation criterion.



9 Rational-mask digital descent beyond pure periodicity

Block 03 verified a four-chart atlas on unit purely periodic sequences. That domain made the linear, rational,
digital, and functional-Mahler charts meet simultaneously, but it left open the larger overlap between rational
functional-Mahler masks and automatic sequences. The present block removes the periodicity hypothesis while
keeping the normalization and bridge standards unchanged.
Fix a prime 𝑝. Let                                                                           
                                                                         𝐹 (𝑧)
                                 RMah×𝑝 =       𝐹 (𝑧) ∈ 1 + 𝑧F 𝑝 [[𝑧]] :          ∈ F 𝑝 (𝑧)
                                                                         𝐹 (𝑧 𝑝 )
be the discrete category of unit formal series with rational canonical functional-Mahler mask. This category
contains the periodic objects of Section 8, but for odd 𝑝 it also contains genuinely nonrational and non-C-finite
automatic series.

9.1 Frobenius-radical characterization

For a unit formal series, define its canonical local mask by

                                                              𝐹 (𝑧)
                                                  𝑅𝐹 (𝑧) =             .                                         (18)
                                                              𝐹 (𝑧 𝑝 )
The coefficientwise infinite product                            Ö  𝑗
                                              M 𝑝 [𝑅] (𝑧) =      𝑅 𝑧𝑝                                            (19)
                                                                𝑗 ≥0

is well defined for every 𝑅 ∈ 1 + 𝑧F 𝑝 [[𝑧]], because only finitely many factors contribute to a fixed coefficient.

Theorem 9.1 (Frobenius-radical characterization). Let 𝐹 ∈ 1 + 𝑧F 𝑝 [[𝑧]]. Then

                                                 𝑅𝐹 (𝑧) = 𝐹 (𝑧) 1− 𝑝 .                                           (20)

Consequently, the following are equivalent.
 (i) The canonical mask 𝑅𝐹 is rational.
(ii) The power 𝐹 𝑝−1 belongs to F 𝑝 (𝑧).
(iii) There are coprime polynomials 𝐴, 𝐵 ∈ F 𝑝 [𝑧] with

                                                    𝐴(0) = 𝐵(0) = 1

     and
                                                𝐵(𝑧)𝐹 (𝑧) = 𝐴(𝑧)𝐹 (𝑧 𝑝 ).                                        (21)

For the reduced mask 𝑅𝐹 = 𝐴/𝐵, one has
                                                                 𝐵(𝑧)
                                                 𝐹 (𝑧) 𝑝−1 =          .                                          (22)
                                                                 𝐴(𝑧)
The ratio and product constructions are strict inverses:

                                           𝐹 ↦−→ 𝑅𝐹 ,        𝑅 ↦−→ M 𝑝 [𝑅].

In particular, every object of RMah×𝑝 is algebraic over F 𝑝 (𝑧) of degree at most 𝑝 − 1.

Proof. In characteristic 𝑝, Frobenius gives
                                                  𝐹 (𝑧 𝑝 ) = 𝐹 (𝑧) 𝑝 .
Substitution into Equation (18) gives Equation (20). Thus 𝑅𝐹 is rational exactly when 𝐹 𝑝−1 = 𝑅𝐹−1 is rational.
Writing the reduced rational mask as 𝐴/𝐵 gives Equation (21) and Equation (22).
Conversely, a reduced rational mask 𝐴/𝐵 with unit constant terms defines a unique formal solution of Equation (21):
the product in Equation (19). Telescoping gives

                                                M 𝑝 [𝑅] (𝑧)
                                                               = 𝑅(𝑧),
                                                M 𝑝 [𝑅] (𝑧 𝑝 )

while uniqueness follows by comparing coefficients recursively. Hence the ratio and product constructions are
strict inverses. Finally, Equation (22) is an algebraic equation of degree 𝑝 − 1.                          □

9.2 The rational-collapse obstruction

Let 𝑅 = 𝐴/𝐵 be reduced and normalized. For each monic irreducible polynomial 𝜋 ∈ F 𝑝 [𝑧], define
                                                            
                                𝜔 𝜋 (𝑅) = 𝑣 𝜋 (𝐵) − 𝑣 𝜋 ( 𝐴) mod ( 𝑝 − 1).
Include the valuation at infinity
                                                              
                                        𝜔∞ (𝑅) = deg 𝐴 − deg 𝐵 mod ( 𝑝 − 1).
The finite-support family                                          
                                                𝜔 𝑝−1 (𝑅) = 𝜔 𝑣 (𝑅) 𝑣                                           (23)
is the rational-collapse obstruction.
Theorem 9.3 (Exact rational-collapse criterion). Let 𝐹 = M 𝑝 [ 𝐴/𝐵]. The following are equivalent.
 (i) 𝐹 ∈ F 𝑝 (𝑧).
(ii) 𝐵/𝐴 is a ( 𝑝 − 1)-st power in F 𝑝 (𝑧).
(iii) 𝜔 𝑝−1 ( 𝐴/𝐵) = 0.
When these conditions hold, there is a unique rational function 𝐺 ∈ F 𝑝 (𝑧) with 𝐺 (0) = 1 and
                                                    𝐺 𝑝−1 = 𝐵/𝐴,
and 𝐹 = 𝐺.
If 𝑝 = 2, every rational mask collapses to a rational series. If 𝑝 > 2 and 𝜔 𝑝−1 ( 𝐴/𝐵) ≠ 0, then 𝐹 is nonrational,
non-C-finite, and not eventually periodic.

Proof. The equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows from Equation (22). If 𝐵/𝐴 = 𝐺 𝑝−1 , every valuation of 𝐵/𝐴 is
divisible by 𝑝 − 1, so (ii) implies (iii).
Conversely, suppose all valuation residues vanish. Since 𝐴 and 𝐵 are coprime, every irreducible exponent in each
polynomial is divisible by 𝑝 − 1. Factoring into monic irreducibles therefore gives
                                              𝐴 = 𝐶 𝑝−1 ,        𝐵 = 𝐷 𝑝−1
for polynomials 𝐶, 𝐷 after a constant normalization. The unit conditions 𝐴(0) = 𝐵(0) = 1 remove the remaining
scalar obstruction. Thus 𝐵/𝐴 = (𝐷/𝐶) 𝑝−1 . Multiplying 𝐷/𝐶 by its inverse constant term does not change its
( 𝑝 − 1)-st power, and gives the unique root with constant term one. The formal solution is unique, so 𝐹 = 𝐺.
For 𝑝 = 2, the exponent 𝑝 − 1 is one, hence the obstruction group is trivial and 𝐹 = 𝐵/𝐴. Over a finite field, a
rational coefficient series is C-finite and eventually periodic, while an eventually periodic sequence has a rational
generating function. Therefore a nonzero obstruction forces all three failures stated in the theorem.              □

Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                                                  Komorebi P.

Definition 9.5 (Canonical Cartier module and Cartier rank). The canonical Cartier module of 𝐹 is
                                                                                

Its dimension
                                                 𝑟 𝑝 (𝐹) = dimF 𝑝 V𝑝 (𝐹)                                          (31)
is the Cartier rank.

Theorem 9.6 (Minimality of the canonical Cartier module). For every 𝐹 ∈ RMah×𝑝 , the module C 𝑝 (𝐹) is finite
dimensional, reachable, and observable. It is therefore a minimal digit-linear realization of the coefficient function.
Every other reachable-observable digit-linear realization of the same coefficient function is uniquely similar to
C 𝑝 (𝐹).

Proof. By theorem 9.4, every section Λ𝑤 𝐹 lies in the (𝐷 + 1)-dimensional image of Θ 𝐴,𝐵 . Hence V𝑝 (𝐹) is finite
dimensional. Reachability is built into its definition as the span of the orbit of 𝐹.
For observability, let 𝐺 ∈ V𝑝 (𝐹) satisfy
                                                      𝜖 0 (Λ𝑤 𝐺) = 0
for every digit word 𝑤. For any 𝑛 ≥ 0, choose the base-𝑝 digits of 𝑛. The section identity gives

                                                 [𝑧 𝑛 ]𝐺 = 𝜖 0 (Λ𝑤 𝐺) = 0.

Thus 𝐺 = 0, so the observation family separates points.
For uniqueness, let (𝑉, (𝐿 𝑑 ), 𝑣, 𝜆) be another reachable-observable realization of the same digit function. Send the
reachable vector 𝐿 𝑤 𝑣 to Λ𝑤 𝐹. If a linear combination of reachable vectors vanishes, applying every continuation
and then 𝜆 shows that the corresponding combination of Cartier sections has every coefficient zero. Observability
makes the assignment well defined. Reachability makes it surjective, and the symmetric construction gives an
inverse. The intertwining, initial-vector, and output identities are immediate. Any such similarity is determined on
the reachable spanning set, so it is unique.                                                                         □

Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                      Komorebi P.


9.4 The exact reachable compiler core

Let R ( 𝐴, 𝐵) ⊆ P𝐷 be the finite set of numerator states reachable from 𝐵 under the transitions 𝑇𝑑 , and let

                                            𝑊 ( 𝐴, 𝐵) = spanF 𝑝 R ( 𝐴, 𝐵).

Theorem 9.7 (Reachable numerator core). The map Θ 𝐴,𝐵 restricts to
                                                             ∼
                                                 R ( 𝐴, 𝐵) −→ K 𝑝 (𝐹)                                            (32)

and to a linear isomorphism
                                                             ∼
                                                𝑊 ( 𝐴, 𝐵) −→ V𝑝 (𝐹).                                             (33)
The reachable deterministic output automaton of the fixed-denominator compiler is already minimal. No nontrivial
output-equivalence quotient remains after reachability restriction.

Proof. For every word 𝑤, repeated use of Equation (27) gives

                                                 Θ 𝐴,𝐵 (𝑇𝑤 𝐵) = Λ𝑤 𝐹.

Thus Θ 𝐴,𝐵 maps the reachable numerator set onto the Cartier kernel. Injectivity of Θ 𝐴,𝐵 makes the map bijective,
and taking linear spans gives Equation (33).
If two reachable numerators 𝑃 ≠ 𝑄 had identical future output behavior, the two series Θ(𝑃) and Θ(𝑄) would
have identical coefficients and would therefore be equal. Injectivity would force 𝑃 = 𝑄, a contradiction. Hence all
reachable states are behaviorally distinct. Reachability and distinguishability prove minimality.                □

The preceding theorem sharpens the generic reduction language used in Block 03. A behavioral quotient remains
a safe general construction for arbitrary output automata, but it is trivial for the normalized fixed-denominator
numerator compiler.
Let
                                                  𝜅 𝑝 (𝐹) = |K 𝑝 (𝐹)|.                                           (34)
Corollary 9.8 (Exact state-complexity hierarchy). For a reduced rational mask 𝐴/𝐵,

                                      𝑟 𝑝 (𝐹) ≤ 𝜅 𝑝 (𝐹) ≤ 𝑝 𝑟 𝑝 (𝐹 ) ≤ 𝑝 𝐷 ( 𝐴,𝐵)+1 .                            (35)

Moreover,
                                 𝑟 𝑝 (𝐹) = dim 𝑊 ( 𝐴, 𝐵),         𝜅 𝑝 (𝐹) = |R ( 𝐴, 𝐵)|.

Proof. The Cartier kernel spans V𝑝 (𝐹), so its cardinality is at least the dimension. It is a subset of the 𝑝 𝑟 𝑝 (𝐹 )
vectors in V𝑝 (𝐹). Finally, V𝑝 (𝐹) embeds into the (𝐷 + 1)-dimensional compiler space by theorem 9.7.               □

Theorem 9.9 (Rational-mask three-chart equivalence). The semantic functors induce equivalences of groupoids

                                             Maskrat     min,rat
                                                 𝑝 ≃ Cart 𝑝      ≃ Autmin,rat
                                                                      𝑝       ≃ RMah×𝑝 .                                                          (36)

The mask-series bridge is a strict inverse pair. The Cartier and digital bridges are normalized equivalences with
unique comparison isomorphisms.

Proof. The strict mask-series equivalence is theorem 9.1. The canonical Cartier construction gives an object of the
second chart, and theorem 9.6 gives a unique similarity from every other minimal realization of the same series.
The canonical 𝑝-kernel automaton is finite by theorem 9.4. It is reachable by construction. Distinct kernel series
differ in some coefficient, and the digit word for that coefficient distinguishes their future outputs, so the automaton
is minimal. The usual behavioral map gives the unique isomorphism from any other reachable minimal DFAO for
the same series.                                                                                                       □

Proof. The reduced canonical mask is unique. Any automorphism of a reachable Cartier module that fixes the
initial vector and commutes with all digit operators fixes a spanning set, hence is the identity. Any automorphism
of a reachable deterministic automaton that fixes the start state and commutes with every digit transition fixes every
state, hence is the identity. Existence and uniqueness of isomorphisms between two normalized realizations follow
from theorems 9.6 and 9.9.                                                                                          □

Let ℜrm
     𝑝 denote the three-chart pseudofunctor defined by the passports above.

Theorem 9.11 (Rational-mask coherence and effective descent). Choose every pairwise bridge by the canonical
constructions and every comparison cell by the unique normalized isomorphism over the common series. Then the
triple-overlap coherence law holds, and
                                                              ×
                                              Desc(ℜrm
                                                    𝑝 ) ≃ RMah 𝑝 .                                               (37)

Descent is effective for objects and morphisms.

Proof. On the only nonempty triple overlap, both composites are isomorphisms between normalized presentations
of the same series. By theorem 9.10, the isomorphism is unique, so the comparison diagram commutes.
Given 𝐹 ∈ RMah×𝑝 , its reduced mask, canonical Cartier module, and minimal 𝑝-kernel automaton form descent
data. Conversely, compatible local objects have one common semantic series. Fiber rigidity identifies them
uniquely with its normalized presentations. The same argument applies to morphisms. Hence the normalization
functor is fully faithful and essentially surjective.                                                     □

This theorem is strictly broader than theorem 8.10: no periodicity or C-finiteness hypothesis is imposed. It is also
strictly narrower than an atlas of all automatic sequences, because an automatic series may have a nonrational
canonical mask.


9.7 Compatible invariants and collapse stratification

For 𝐹 ∈ RMah×𝑝 , define
                                                                                                  
                  J𝑝rm (𝐹) = 𝑅𝐹 , 𝜔 𝑝−1 (𝑅𝐹 ), 𝐷 (𝑅𝐹 ), V𝑝 (𝐹), 𝑟 𝑝 (𝐹), K 𝑝 (𝐹), 𝜅 𝑝 (𝐹), A 𝑝 (𝐹) .             (38)

Theorem 9.12 (Invariant descent on the rational-mask atlas). The native mask, Cartier-module, and automaton
invariants admit coherent comparison maps to J𝑝rm . They descend to the category in Equation (37). The descended
invariant is complete on RMah×𝑝 .

Proof. The fixed-denominator intertwining theorem identifies the reachable numerator span with V𝑝 (𝐹) and the
reachable numerator set with K 𝑝 (𝐹). Hence all components of the package agree under the bridges. Coherence
follows from theorem 9.11. If two packages agree, their reduced canonical masks agree. The coefficientwise mask
product then reconstructs the same formal series, so the semantic objects are equal.                          □

The obstruction 𝜔 𝑝−1 divides the semantic category into two exact loci:

                                       RMah×𝑝 = RMah×𝑝,col ⊔ RMah×𝑝,ncol .

The collapse locus consists of rational, hence ultimately periodic, series. The noncollapse locus consists of
non-C-finite automatic series. The unit-periodic atlas of Block 03 is the persistent, zero-transient subatlas inside
RMah×𝑝,col .

9.9 Exact finite verification

The Block 04 verifier enumerates every reduced unit mask with

          deg 𝐴, deg 𝐵 ≤ 3      ( 𝑝 = 2),      deg 𝐴, deg 𝐵 ≤ 2     ( 𝑝 = 3),       deg 𝐴, deg 𝐵 ≤ 1     ( 𝑝 = 5).

For every mask it checks, using exact finite-field and polynomial arithmetic:
 1. the invariant degree bound on the full ambient numerator space;
 2. the functional-equation coefficient recursion against compiler output;
 3. transition closure and fixed-denominator intertwining;
 4. distinguishability of every pair of reachable numerator states;
 5. the identities
                                        𝑊 ( 𝐴, 𝐵)  V𝑝 (𝐹),         R ( 𝐴, 𝐵)  K 𝑝 (𝐹);

 6. the state hierarchy in Equation (35);
 7. characteristic-two collapse and the named collapse or noncollapse examples.
The finite calculation validates the implementation on the recorded ranges. It is not used as a substitute for the
unbounded proofs.

  Block 04 result. The verified heterogeneous atlas now extends beyond pure periodicity. Every unit rational
  functional-Mahler mask over F 𝑝 has a finite canonical Cartier module and a finite canonical 𝑝-kernel
  automaton. The three normalized charts satisfy coherence and effective descent. The fixed-denominator
  compiler has an exact reachable core: reachable numerator states are already the minimal digital states,
  and their span is the minimal Cartier module. The rational-collapse obstruction separates the ultimately
  periodic sublocus from genuinely non-C-finite automatic series. The resulting invariant package is globally
  complete on the declared rational-mask semantic category, but no claim is made for automatic sequences
  whose canonical mask is nonrational.



10 Fixed-radix regular normalization and the automatic finite-orbit boundary

Block 04 closes a substantial automatic slice, but its semantic domain is still defined by the existence of a rational
functional-Mahler mask. The next digital question is more basic and more general: what is the canonical normalized


                                                             62
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                                                  Komorebi P.


representation of a sequence whose complete fixed-radix section orbit spans a finite-dimensional vector space,
even when that orbit itself is infinite?
This section answers that question over a field. It separates two invariants that coincide only in special cases:

                               linear section rank             and        cardinality of the section orbit.

The first controls regularity and minimal digit-linear dimension. The second controls automaticity and minimal
deterministic-state cardinality. Their exact relationship is the automatic–regular boundary developed below.


         Fixed-radix regular normalization and the automatic finite-orbit boundary

10.1 Section operators and the canonical kernel module

Fix an integer radix 𝑘 ≥ 2 and a field 𝐾. Let

                                                          Σ 𝑘 = {0, 1, . . . , 𝑘 − 1}.

Words are read from least significant digit to most significant digit. For

                                                         𝑤 = 𝑑0 𝑑1 · · · 𝑑ℓ −1 ∈ Σ∗𝑘 ,

define
                                                                                  ℓ −1
                                                                                  ∑︁
                                                     |𝑤| = ℓ,          [𝑤] 𝑘 =           𝑑𝑗𝑘 𝑗.
                                                                                  𝑗=0

The empty word 𝜀 has length zero and value zero.
For a sequence 𝑎 = (𝑎 𝑛 ) 𝑛≥0 ∈ 𝐾 N , define the digit-section operators

                                               (Λ𝑑 𝑎) (𝑛) = 𝑎(𝑘𝑛 + 𝑑),                0 ≤ 𝑑 < 𝑘.

                                                                        63
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                       Komorebi P.


For a word 𝑤 = 𝑑0 · · · 𝑑ℓ −1 , put
                                                  Λ𝑤 = Λ𝑑ℓ −1 · · · Λ𝑑1 Λ𝑑0 .
Then
                                               (Λ𝑤 𝑎) (𝑛) = 𝑎 𝑘 |𝑤 | 𝑛 + [𝑤] 𝑘 .
                                                                              
                                                                                                                   (39)
Definition 10.1 (Fixed-radix kernel and section module). The 𝑘-kernel and its linear span are
                               K𝑘 (𝑎) = {Λ𝑤 𝑎 : 𝑤 ∈ Σ∗𝑘 },            V𝑘 (𝑎) = span𝐾 K𝑘 (𝑎).
The sequence is 𝑘-regular over 𝐾 when V𝑘 (𝑎) is finite dimensional. Its regular rank is
                                                    𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) = dim𝐾 V𝑘 (𝑎).
It is 𝑘-automatic in the present reading convention when K𝑘 (𝑎) is finite. In that case set
                                                      𝜅 𝑘 (𝑎) = |K𝑘 (𝑎)|.

The terminology records two different finiteness statements. Regularity asks for a finite-dimensional linear hull.
Automaticity asks for a finite orbit under the section semigroup itself.
Definition 10.2 (Digit-linear realization). A digit-linear realization of 𝑎 is a tuple
                                                L = (𝑊, 𝐴0 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘−1 , 𝑣, 𝜆),
where 𝑊 is a finite-dimensional 𝐾-vector space, 𝐴𝑑 ∈ End𝐾 (𝑊), 𝑣 ∈ 𝑊, and 𝜆 ∈ 𝑊 ∗ , such that for every word
𝑤 = 𝑑0 · · · 𝑑ℓ −1 ,
                                  𝑎 [𝑤 ] 𝑘 = 𝜆𝐴𝑤 𝑣,   𝐴𝑤 = 𝐴𝑑ℓ −1 · · · 𝐴𝑑0 .                            (40)
Because different words can represent the same integer after terminal high zeros are appended, equation (40)
includes the required leading-zero consistency.
Theorem 10.3 (Section-module realization theorem). For a sequence 𝑎 ∈ 𝐾 N , the following are equivalent:
 1. 𝑎 is 𝑘-regular over 𝐾;
 2. 𝑎 admits a finite-dimensional digit-linear realization.
When these conditions hold, the tuple
                                                                           
                              C 𝑘 (𝑎) = V𝑘 (𝑎), Λ0 , . . . , Λ 𝑘−1 , 𝑎, 𝜖 0 ,         𝜖0 ( 𝑓 ) = 𝑓 (0),            (41)
is a canonical realization of dimension 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎).

Proof. Assume first that V𝑘 (𝑎) is finite dimensional. It is invariant under every Λ𝑑 by construction. Choose any
basis, represent the section operators by matrices, take the initial vector to be 𝑎, and take output to be evaluation at
zero. For every word 𝑤,
                                          𝜖 0 (Λ𝑤 𝑎) = (Λ𝑤 𝑎) (0) = 𝑎 [𝑤 ] 𝑘 ,
so the resulting realization satisfies (40).
Conversely, suppose 𝑎 has a realization (𝑊, 𝐴𝑑 , 𝑣, 𝜆). For 𝑥 ∈ 𝑊, define its represented behavior by
                                                   B (𝑥) (𝑛) = 𝜆𝐴dig𝑘 (𝑛) 𝑥,
where 𝐴dig𝑘 (𝑛) is the product associated with the canonical least-significant-digit-first expansion of 𝑛. Equation
(40) and concatenation of digit words give
                                                 Λ𝑤 𝑎 = B ( 𝐴𝑤 𝑣).
Hence every kernel section belongs to B (𝑊), so
                                                  dim𝐾 V𝑘 (𝑎) ≤ dim𝐾 𝑊 .
Thus 𝑎 is regular. The realization in (41) has dimension exactly 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎).                                             □

                                                               64
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                           Komorebi P.


Proposition 10.4 (Reachability and observability). The canonical section realization C 𝑘 (𝑎) is linearly reachable
and observable:
                                      span𝐾 {Λ𝑤 𝑎 : 𝑤 ∈ Σ∗𝑘 } = V𝑘 (𝑎),
and                                                  Ù
                                                            ker(𝜖 0 Λ𝑤 ) = {0}.
                                                   𝑤 ∈Σ∗𝑘


Proof. Reachability is the definition of V𝑘 (𝑎). If 𝑓 ∈ V𝑘 (𝑎) satisfies (𝜖 0 Λ𝑤 ) ( 𝑓 ) = 0 for every word 𝑤, then

                                                            𝑓 ( [𝑤] 𝑘 ) = 0

for every word. Every nonnegative integer has such an expansion, so 𝑓 = 0.                                               □


10.2 Word-Hankel rank and minimal digit-linear dimension

The canonical module can be read directly from a Hankel-type matrix indexed by digit words.
Definition 10.5 (Word-Hankel matrix). The fixed-radix word-Hankel matrix of 𝑎 is the infinite matrix

                              𝐻 𝑎 (𝑢, 𝑣) = 𝑎 [𝑢] 𝑘 +𝑘 |𝑢| [𝑣 ] 𝑘 = (Λ𝑢 𝑎) ( [𝑣] 𝑘 ),   𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ Σ∗𝑘 .                    (42)

Theorem 10.6 (Regular rank theorem). Let 𝑎 be 𝑘-regular over 𝐾. Then

                 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) = rank𝐾 𝐻 𝑎 = min{dim𝐾 𝑊 : 𝑊 supports a digit-linear realization of 𝑎}.                       (43)

Moreover:
 1. there exist finite word sets 𝑈, 𝑉 ⊂ Σ∗𝑘 , each of cardinality 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎), for which the minor 𝐻 𝑎 [𝑈, 𝑉] is nonsingular;
 2. every reachable-observable realization of dimension 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) is isomorphic to C 𝑘 (𝑎);
 3. any two minimal reachable-observable realizations are related by a unique similarity intertwining all digit
    operators, the initial vector, and the output functional.

Proof. The row indexed by 𝑢 in 𝐻 𝑎 is the behavior of the section Λ𝑢 𝑎 evaluated on all continuation words. By
observability in theorem 10.4, evaluation on all words is injective on V𝑘 (𝑎). Therefore the row span of 𝐻 𝑎 is
naturally isomorphic to V𝑘 (𝑎), and
                                              rank𝐾 𝐻 𝑎 = 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎).

For an arbitrary realization (𝑊, 𝐴𝑑 , 𝑣 in , 𝜆), concatenation gives the factorization

                                                    𝐻 𝑎 (𝑢, 𝑣) = 𝜆𝐴𝑣 𝐴𝑢 𝑣 in .

Thus 𝐻 𝑎 factors through 𝑊, so rank 𝐻 𝑎 ≤ dim 𝑊. The canonical realization has dimension 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎), proving the
minimality formula.
An infinite matrix of finite rank 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) has a nonsingular finite 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) × 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) minor, which gives the first assertion.
For the remaining assertions, define the behavior map of any realization by

                                                  B𝑊 (𝑥) (𝑛) = 𝜆𝐴dig𝑘 (𝑛) 𝑥.

Observability makes this map injective. Reachability and equality of the represented sequence make its image
equal to V𝑘 (𝑎). In minimal dimension it is therefore an isomorphism intertwining the digit operators, initial vector,
and output. Composing the two behavior isomorphisms gives the unique similarity between any two minimal
reachable-observable realizations.                                                                                  □

                                                                 65
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                                                     Komorebi P.



 The regular rank is simultaneously a section-module, Hankel, and minimal-state dimension

             rows are section behaviors                             rank rk(a)                             columns separate sections

                                        det Ha[U, V] ≠ 0                                        Ha = PQ

Figure 20: Three exact meanings of the regular rank. The finite nonsingular minor is a certificate rather than an
approximation: it proves a lower bound that is attained by the canonical section module.


Remark 10.7 (Finite certification). The theorem is infinite in statement but finite in certificate. A proposed rank 𝑟
can be certified by:
 1. an explicit 𝑟-dimensional section-stable span containing 𝑎, which proves 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) ≤ 𝑟;
 2. an explicit nonsingular 𝑟 × 𝑟 word-Hankel minor, which proves 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) ≥ 𝑟.
When both are supplied, the regular rank is exact.


10.3 Strict shortlex normalization

Minimal realizations are unique up to similarity, but the stratified atlas needs a literal normalized chart rather than
an unnamed similarity class. The actual section functions supply a canonical basis choice.
Order Σ∗𝑘 by shortlex order: shorter words precede longer words, and words of equal length are ordered
lexicographically. Starting from the empty list, scan the words in this order and retain a word precisely when its
section is not in the span of the previously retained sections.

Definition 10.8 (Greedy shortlex kernel basis). For a regular sequence 𝑎, let

                                                           𝑈𝑎 = (𝑢 1 , . . . , 𝑢𝑟𝑘 (𝑎) )

be the unique ordered list produced by the greedy shortlex rule, and put

                                                                   𝑏 𝑖 = Λ𝑢𝑖 𝑎.

For the zero sequence, the list is empty.

Because membership in a linear span is intrinsic, the resulting ordered basis depends only on the represented
sequence, the field, the radix, and the fixed word order.



                                                                         66
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                        Komorebi P.


Construction 10.9 (Strict shortlex realization). Write

                                   𝑘 (𝑎)
                                  𝑟∑︁                               ∑︁
                       Λ𝑑 𝑏 𝑗 =            (𝑀𝑑 (𝑎))𝑖 𝑗 𝑏 𝑖 ,   𝑎=        𝑣 𝑖 (𝑎)𝑏 𝑖 ,   ℓ𝑖 (𝑎) = 𝑏 𝑖 (0).
                                   𝑖=1                              𝑖

The strict shortlex record is
                                                                                             
                                  SL 𝑘,𝐾 (𝑎) = 𝑈𝑎 , 𝑀0 (𝑎), . . . , 𝑀𝑘−1 (𝑎), 𝑣(𝑎), ℓ(𝑎) .                         (44)

Theorem 10.10 (Strict fixed-radix normal form). The record SL 𝑘,𝐾 (𝑎) is uniquely determined by 𝑎 and reconstructs
𝑎 exactly through
                                            𝑎 [𝑤 ] 𝑘 = ℓ(𝑎)𝑀𝑤 (𝑎)𝑣(𝑎).
It has dimension 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎). Two regular sequences have the same strict shortlex record if and only if they are equal.

Proof. The greedy word list is uniquely determined by the actual section functions. Once the ordered basis is fixed,
the coefficients of every digit section, the initial vector, and the output functional are unique. The reconstruction
identity is the coordinate form of the canonical section realization. Equality of records therefore implies equality of
every coefficient, while equality of sequences gives the same sections and hence the same greedy record.             □

Theorem 10.11 (Fixed-radix regular normalized atlas). Let Reg 𝑘 (𝐾) be the discrete semantic category of 𝐾-valued
𝑘-regular sequences. Let Seccan                                                                            sl
                               𝑘 (𝐾) be the category of canonical section-module records (41), and let Lin 𝑘,𝐾 be
the category of strict shortlex records (44), with morphisms required to preserve the represented sequence. Then

                                                Reg 𝑘 (𝐾) ≃ Seccan        sl
                                                               𝑘 (𝐾) ≃ Lin 𝑘,𝐾 .                                   (45)

Every normalized fiber over a fixed sequence is contractible. Consequently the associated finite presentation atlas
has effective object and morphism descent.

Proof. A sequence determines its canonical section module and its strict shortlex record functorially. Conversely,
either record reconstructs every coefficient. The two composites are identities because the section module is
defined from the represented sequence and the shortlex basis is intrinsic. The uniqueness in theorem 10.10
leaves no nontrivial automorphism in a normalized fiber. Compatible local objects and morphisms therefore glue
uniquely.                                                                                                       □

Definition 10.12 (Fixed-radix regular invariant). The normalized fixed-radix invariant is
                                 reg                                                
                               J𝑘,𝐾 (𝑎) = 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎), C 𝑘 (𝑎), SL 𝑘,𝐾 (𝑎), [𝐻 𝑎 [𝑈, 𝑉]] ,                             (46)

where [𝐻 𝑎 [𝑈, 𝑉]] denotes any recorded nonsingular rank certificate together with its word addresses. The strict
normal form makes this package complete on Reg 𝑘 (𝐾).

The finite Hankel minor is not canonical unless an ordering rule is imposed on candidate column sets. It is therefore
treated as a certificate attached to the canonical invariant, not as the invariant itself.


10.4 The automatic finite-orbit subatlas

The automatic locus is not a second unrelated theory. It is the locus on which the orbit of the distinguished initial
vector inside the canonical regular module is finite.




                                                               67
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                    Komorebi P.


Theorem 10.13 (Kernel automaton theorem). Assume K𝑘 (𝑎) is finite. Define a deterministic automaton with
output by
                                                 𝑑
                       state set K𝑘 (𝑎),       𝑓 −
                                                 → Λ𝑑 𝑓 ,         initial state 𝑎,   output 𝑓 (0).
Then:
 1. the automaton represents 𝑎 under least-significant-digit-first reading;
 2. every state is reachable;
 3. distinct states are behaviorally distinguishable;
 4. it is the unique minimal DFAO up to state renaming.

Proof. After reading a word 𝑤, the state is Λ𝑤 𝑎, whose output is 𝑎 [𝑤 ] 𝑘 . Reachability is immediate from the
definition of the kernel. If 𝑓 ≠ 𝑔, then there is an integer 𝑛 with 𝑓 (𝑛) ≠ 𝑔(𝑛). Reading a digit word for 𝑛 from
either state distinguishes them. Thus no two states can be merged, and the reachable automaton is minimal. The
usual residual-behavior argument then gives uniqueness up to renaming.                                          □

Theorem 10.14 (Automatic–regular finite-orbit equivalence). For a 𝑘-regular sequence 𝑎, the following are
equivalent:
 1. 𝑎 is 𝑘-automatic;
 2. the orbit {Λ𝑤 𝑎 : 𝑤 ∈ Σ∗𝑘 } of the initial vector in C 𝑘 (𝑎) is finite;
 3. the orbit of 𝑣(𝑎) under the strict shortlex digit matrices is finite.
On this locus, the minimal DFAO is recovered as the orbit itself. Hence

                           Aut 𝑘 (𝐾) ≃ FinOrbSec 𝑘 (𝐾) ≃ FinOrbLinsl𝑘,𝐾 ≃ DFAOmin
                                                                              𝑘,𝐾 .                            (47)

If 𝐾 = F𝑞 is finite, every 𝑘-regular 𝐾-valued sequence is automatic.

Proof. The first three conditions are the same orbit statement written in semantic, intrinsic-module, and coordinate
form. The kernel automaton in theorem 10.13 recovers the deterministic chart, while its state-to-section map
recovers the finite orbit inside the canonical module. These constructions are inverse on normalized objects. If
𝐾 = F𝑞 and 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) < ∞, the vector space V𝑘 (𝑎) has only 𝑞 𝑟𝑘 (𝑎) elements, so the section orbit is automatically
finite.                                                                                                           □

Corollary 10.15 (State-rank hierarchy). Let 𝑎 be automatic with values in F𝑞 . Then

                                                𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) ≤ 𝜅 𝑘 (𝑎) ≤ 𝑞 𝑟𝑘 (𝑎) .                                 (48)

The first inequality can be strict: behavior-distinct kernel states may be linearly dependent.

Proof. The kernel states span V𝑘 (𝑎), so their number is at least its dimension. They form a subset of the
𝑞 𝑟𝑘 (𝑎) -element vector space V𝑘 (𝑎), proving the upper bound.                                          □

The corollary explains why deterministic state minimization and linear-state minimization must remain distinct
coordinates. The minimal automaton records the finite orbit as a set with digit action. The regular rank records the
dimension of its linear span. Neither number alone reconstructs the other.

Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                                                                                                        Komorebi P.

10.5 Exact regular examples beyond automaticity

Over an infinite field, finite linear rank does not force a finite section orbit. The following families give exact,
unbounded witnesses.
Theorem 10.16 (Polynomial regular rank in characteristic zero). Let 𝐾 have characteristic zero and let 𝑃 ∈ 𝐾 [𝑥]
be nonzero of degree 𝑚. For the sequence 𝑎 𝑛 = 𝑃(𝑛),

                                                                                                 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) = 𝑚 + 1.

If 𝑚 ≥ 1, its 𝑘-kernel is infinite.

Proof. Every section has the form
                                                                                    (Λ𝑤 𝑎) (𝑛) = 𝑃(𝑘 |𝑤 | 𝑛 + [𝑤] 𝑘 ),
which is a polynomial in 𝑛 of degree at most 𝑚. Hence the section span has dimension at most 𝑚 + 1.
Choose ℓ with 𝑘 ℓ > 𝑚 and consider the 𝑚 + 1 sections

                                                                                      𝑃(𝑘 ℓ 𝑛 + 𝑟),                 0 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ 𝑚.

When expanded in the basis 1, 𝑛, . . . , 𝑛𝑚 , their coefficient functions as functions of 𝑟 have successive degrees
𝑚, 𝑚 − 1, . . . , 0, with nonzero leading coefficients. Evaluation at the distinct points 0, 1, . . . , 𝑚 therefore gives an
invertible generalized Vandermonde matrix. These sections are linearly independent, proving rank 𝑚 + 1.
For 𝑚 ≥ 1, the sections 𝑃(𝑘 ℓ 𝑛 + 𝑟) are distinct as 𝑟 varies through arbitrarily large complete residue ranges, so the
kernel orbit is infinite.                                                                                             □

Corollary 10.17. For 𝑎 𝑛 = 𝑛, one has 𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) = 2 and exactly 𝑘 ℓ distinct sections at depth ℓ. For 𝑎 𝑛 = 𝑛2 , one has
𝑟 𝑘 (𝑎) = 3 and again exactly 𝑘 ℓ distinct sections at depth ℓ.
Theorem 10.18 (Digit-sum regularity). Let 𝑠 𝑘 (𝑛) be the sum of the base-𝑘 digits of 𝑛, regarded as a sequence over
a characteristic-zero field. Then
                                                   𝑟 𝑘 (𝑠 𝑘 ) = 2,
and
                                                                                     K𝑘 (𝑠 𝑘 ) = {𝑠 𝑘 + 𝑐 : 𝑐 ∈ Z ≥0 }.                                                                              (49)

                                                                                                             69
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                                                                        Komorebi P.


At exact depth ℓ, the number of distinct sections is

                                                                                            (𝑘 − 1)ℓ + 1.

Thus 𝑠 𝑘 is 𝑘-regular but not 𝑘-automatic over a characteristic-zero field.

Proof. The digit identity
                                                                                    𝑠 𝑘 (𝑘𝑛 + 𝑑) = 𝑠 𝑘 (𝑛) + 𝑑
gives
                                                                                        Λ𝑑 𝑠 𝑘 = 𝑠 𝑘 + 𝑑.
Iterating shows that every section is 𝑠 𝑘 + 𝑐, so the span is contained in span{1, 𝑠 𝑘 }. The two sequences are
independent, giving rank two. At depth ℓ, the additive constant is the digit sum of an ℓ-digit residue. Every integer
from 0 through (𝑘 − 1)ℓ occurs, giving exactly (𝑘 − 1)ℓ + 1 states.                                                □

10.6 Exact finite verification

The Block 05 verifier performs two complementary exact audits.
First, it exhaustively enumerates bounded deterministic automata in three configurations:
                                 (𝑘, 𝑞, 𝑠max ) = (2, 2, 3),        (2, 3, 3),   (3, 2, 2).
Every candidate is restricted to reachable states, minimized by behavioral equivalence, and filtered by the
terminal-high-zero consistency required by integer semantics. For every resulting normalized automaton the
verifier checks:
 1. reachability and pairwise behavioral distinguishability;
 2. construction of the canonical linear quotient from continuation-output rows;
 3. transition and output equivariance of the state embedding;
 4. the inequalities in Equation (48);
 5. equality of word-Hankel rank and regular rank;
 6. exact equality between the finite linear orbit and the minimized automaton state set;
 7. coefficient agreement on a fixed exact test range.
Second, it checks unbounded symbolic families using exact integer and rational matrices. Polynomial sequences
𝑛𝑚 are checked for bases two and three through degree six, and digit sums are checked in bases two, three, and four,
including their exact depth-orbit growth formulas.
The finite audit records 89,055 exact checks and no failures. It supports the implementation, examples, and
bounded state-rank distribution. The unbounded theorems above are proved symbolically and do not depend on the
enumeration.

  Exact boundary of Block 05. The normalization theorem is fixed-radix and field-valued. It does not yet give
  one integral normal form valid simultaneously modulo every prime power, a semiring minimality theorem, or
  a canonical all-radix comparison invariant. Over a general commutative ring, the section module can have
  torsion, fail to be free, and admit inequivalent minimal generating systems. Saturation, Smith/Fitting data,
  prime-power reduction towers, and local completion therefore remain separate arithmetic obligations rather
  than being hidden inside the field theorem.



                                                              71
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                         Komorebi P.



  Block 05 result. Every field-valued fixed-radix regular sequence has a canonical section module, a minimal
  reachable-observable digit-linear realization, an exact word-Hankel rank, and a strict shortlex normal form.
  These charts satisfy effective normalized descent and yield a complete invariant on the declared fixed-radix
  regular category. The automatic locus is exactly the finite-orbit locus inside the same module, where the
  minimal DFAO is recovered as the orbit of the distinguished initial vector. Over finite fields regularity and
  automaticity coincide, while over characteristic-zero fields polynomial and digit-sum examples have finite
  rank and infinite section orbit. This closes fixed-radix linear-state minimality over fields, not the all-moduli or
  integral regular-sequence problem.



11 Integral saturation, Smith–Fitting defects, and prime-power effectivity

Block 05 proves a complete fixed-radix normalization theorem over a field. The next question is not obtained by
replacing the coefficient field with Z symbol by symbol. Over Z, two canonical lattices appear inside the same
rational section space. Their finite-index difference is invisible over Q, but it controls reduction modulo every
integer, the appearance of ghost coordinates, and the distinction between an effective 𝑝-adic lift and an effective
integral lift.
Throughout this section, 𝑘 ≥ 2 is fixed and
                                                       𝑎 : N −→ Z
is an integer-valued sequence whose rational section space

                                             𝑉Q (𝑎) = spanQ K𝑘 (𝑎) ⊆ QN

has finite dimension
                                                 𝜌 𝑘 (𝑎) = dimQ 𝑉Q (𝑎).
Thus 𝑎 is 𝑘-regular after extension of scalars to Q.


11.1 The raw section lattice and its canonical saturation

Definition 11.1 (Raw and saturated section lattices). The raw section lattice and the saturated section lattice are

                                 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) = spanZ K𝑘 (𝑎),          𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) = 𝑉Q (𝑎) ∩ ZN .

The intersection defining 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) is taken inside QN .

The raw lattice is generated by the actual radix sections. The saturated lattice contains every integer-valued
sequence lying in their rational span. These lattices agree over Q, but need not agree over Z.
Lemma 11.2 (Finite coordinate detection). Let 𝑉 ⊆ QN be a vector space of finite dimension 𝜌. There exist indices

                                                   0 ≤ 𝑛1 < · · · < 𝑛 𝜌

such that the evaluation map
                                                                                           
                                   𝐸 n : 𝑉 −→ Q𝜌 ,        𝑓 ↦−→ 𝑓 (𝑛1 ), . . . , 𝑓 (𝑛𝜌 )

is an isomorphism.

Proof. The coordinate functionals on QN separate points of 𝑉. Choose 𝑛1 for which some element of 𝑉 has nonzero
value. If 𝑛1 , . . . , 𝑛 𝑗 have been chosen and the joint evaluation map has rank 𝑗 < 𝜌, its kernel contains a nonzero 𝑓 .
Choose 𝑛 𝑗+1 with 𝑓 (𝑛 𝑗+1 ) ≠ 0. The rank increases. After 𝜌 steps, the evaluation map has full rank.                 □

                                                           72
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                      Komorebi P.


Theorem 11.3 (Canonical finite saturated section lattice). The groups 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) and 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) are free abelian of rank
𝜌 𝑘 (𝑎). They are stable under every digit section Λ𝑑 , and

                                                    𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) ⊆ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)

has finite index. Moreover, 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) is the unique maximal subgroup of 𝑉Q (𝑎) whose elements are integer-valued.

Proof. Choose a coordinate-detection set n by theorem 11.2. Evaluation embeds 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) into Z𝜌 , so 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) is a
finitely generated free abelian group of rank at most 𝜌. The raw lattice 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) is contained in 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎), and its
rational span is all of 𝑉Q (𝑎). Hence 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) has rank 𝜌, and therefore 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) also has rank 𝜌. A subgroup of a
finitely generated free abelian group is finitely generated and free, so 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) is free of rank 𝜌. Equal-rank lattice
inclusion has finite index.
Every Λ𝑑 preserves 𝑉Q (𝑎) and preserves integer-valued sequences. Hence it preserves 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎). It also maps each
radix section to another radix section, so it preserves 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎). Finally, any integer-valued subgroup of 𝑉Q (𝑎) lies in
𝑉Q (𝑎) ∩ ZN = 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎).                                                                                              □

Definition 11.4 (Integral saturation defect). The finite abelian group

                                                𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎) = 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑀𝑘 (𝑎)

is the integral saturation defect. Its order

                                               𝛿 𝑘 (𝑎) = [𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) : 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎)]

is the saturation index.

By the Smith normal form, there are uniquely determined integers

                                           1 ≤ 𝑑1 (𝑎) | 𝑑2 (𝑎) | · · · | 𝑑 𝜌 (𝑎)

such that
                                                           𝜌
                                                           Ê
                                               𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎)          Z/𝑑𝑖 (𝑎)Z.
                                                           𝑖=1

Factors with 𝑑𝑖 = 1 are harmless but are retained so that the list has length 𝜌.

Example 11.5 (A rank-two nontrivial defect). Let 𝑠 𝑘 (𝑛) be the base-𝑘 digit-sum sequence and let 𝑎 = 2𝑠 𝑘 . Since

                                                    Λ𝑑 𝑠 𝑘 = 𝑠 𝑘 + 𝑑,

the rational section space is spanned by 𝑠 𝑘 and the constant sequence 1. The actual sections of 2𝑠 𝑘 generate

                                               𝑀𝑘 (2𝑠 𝑘 ) = 2Z 𝑠 𝑘 ⊕ 2Z 1,

whereas
                                                 𝐿 𝑘 (2𝑠 𝑘 ) = Z 𝑠 𝑘 ⊕ Z 1.
Thus
                                                 𝐶 𝑘 (2𝑠 𝑘 )  (Z/2Z) 2 .
The rational regular rank is two, but reduction modulo two loses the entire semantic lattice.

The finite quotient is invisible over Q, but it controls every modular reduction.


11.2 The exact reduction sequence

For an integer 𝑚 ≥ 2, the raw coefficient reduction is

                                                        𝑀𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝑀𝑘 (𝑎),

while the semantic reduction inside the saturated lattice is

                               𝑀 𝑘,𝑚 (𝑎) = im (𝑀𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)) .

These need not agree.
Definition 11.6 (Ghost and missing reduction modules). Define

                               𝐺 𝑘,𝑚 (𝑎) = ker (𝑀𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎))

and
                             𝑄 𝑘,𝑚 (𝑎) = coker (𝑀𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)) .
The first records raw residue coordinates that represent the zero semantic sequence. The second records saturated
residue classes not reached by raw sections.
Theorem 11.7 (Smith–Tor reduction sequence). For every 𝑚 ≥ 2, tensoring

                                         0 −→ 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 0

with Z/𝑚Z gives a canonical digit-equivariant exact sequence

             0 −→ 𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎) [𝑚] −→ 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎) −→ 0.

Consequently,
                              𝐺 𝑘,𝑚 (𝑎)  𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎) [𝑚],          𝑄 𝑘,𝑚 (𝑎)  𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎)/𝑚𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎).

                                                              74
Stratified Universality for Resolved Representations                                                           Komorebi P.


Proof. Both 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) and 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) are free abelian. The long exact Tor sequence therefore begins

                        0 −→ TorZ1 (𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎), Z/𝑚Z) −→ 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) ⊗ Z/𝑚Z −→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) ⊗ Z/𝑚Z.

For any abelian group 𝐶,
                                 TorZ1 (𝐶, Z/𝑚Z)  𝐶 [𝑚],            𝐶 ⊗ Z/𝑚Z  𝐶/𝑚𝐶.
The digit-section maps preserve the original short exact sequence, so naturality of Tor makes the five-term sequence
digit-equivariant.                                                                                                 □

                                                                     𝜌

and

The reduction map is an isomorphism if and only if
                                                                 
                                                   gcd 𝑚, 𝑑 𝜌 (𝑎) = 1.

Equivalently, every prime dividing a reduction defect divides the saturation index.

Proof. Choose Smith bases in which the inclusion 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) ↩→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) is the diagonal matrix

                                                 𝐷 = diag(𝑑1 , . . . , 𝑑 𝜌 ).

Modulo 𝑚, the kernel and cokernel of multiplication by 𝑑𝑖 are both cyclic of order gcd(𝑚, 𝑑𝑖 ), and the image has
order 𝑚/gcd(𝑚, 𝑑𝑖 ). The map is invertible in every coordinate exactly when every 𝑑𝑖 is a unit modulo 𝑚, which is
equivalent to gcd(𝑚, 𝑑 𝜌 ) = 1.                                                                                □

Proposition 11.9 (Fitting-ideal certificate). For 0 ≤ 𝑗 < 𝜌,
                                                                                      
                                      Fitt 𝑗 (𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎)) = 𝑑1 (𝑎)𝑑2 (𝑎) · · · 𝑑 𝜌− 𝑗 (𝑎) ,

and
                                            Fitt 𝑗 (𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎)) = Z        ( 𝑗 ≥ 𝜌).
Thus the Smith profile and the Fitting filtration contain the same reduction-defect data at different resolutions.

Proof. Use the diagonal presentation 𝐷 : Z𝜌 → Z𝜌 . The 𝑗-th Fitting ideal is generated by the minors of size 𝜌 − 𝑗.
Since 𝑑1 | · · · | 𝑑 𝜌 , the product of the first 𝜌 − 𝑗 diagonal entries divides every other such minor and is itself one of
the minors.                                                                                                               □

Boundary 11.10 (What the defect classifies). The group 𝐶 𝑘 (𝑎) classifies the finite-index failure of the raw section
lattice to be saturated. It does not classify the whole integral digit module, the sequence, or its section orbit. Two
nonisomorphic digit modules can have the same Smith profile.

11.3 Digit equivariance and the prime-power tower

Choose Smith bases for 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) and 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎), and let

                                                   𝐷 = diag(𝑑1 , . . . , 𝑑 𝜌 )

be the inclusion matrix. Let 𝐴𝑑 and 𝐵 𝑑 be the integer matrices of Λ𝑑 on the raw and saturated lattices.

Proposition 11.11 (Intertwining across saturation). For every digit 𝑑,

                                                        𝐵 𝑑 𝐷 = 𝐷 𝐴𝑑 .

Consequently, the raw modules, semantic images, ghost modules, and missing modules at every modulus form
compatible digit modules.

Proof. The inclusion 𝑀𝑘 (𝑎) ↩→ 𝐿 𝑘 (𝑎) commutes with Λ𝑑 . The displayed matrix identity is that commutative
square in the chosen bases. Reduction modulo 𝑚, passage to kernel, image, and cokernel preserve the induced digit
action.                                                                                                        □

Fix a prime 𝑝 and write
                                                𝜈𝑖 = 𝜈 𝑝,𝑖 (𝑎) = 𝑣 𝑝 (𝑑𝑖 (𝑎)).

In particular, 𝑝 is a good reduction prime exactly when every 𝜈𝑖 = 0.

Proof. Apply theorem 11.8 to 𝑚 = 𝑝 𝑟 . The 𝑖-th semantic image is the ideal

                                                           sem            sem

Proof. The semantic section module is finite, so its orbit under the digit maps is finite. As in the field-valued proof

word at level 𝑟, so the transition map on orbit states is surjective.                                                □

Corollary 11.15 (Good-prime transfer). If 𝑝 ∤ 𝛿 𝑘 (𝑎), then for every 𝑟 ≥ 1,
                                                                   ∼

At a good prime, the raw and saturated prime-power towers agree exactly.

The finite ghosts are therefore real finite-level defects, but they do not survive as nonzero points of the completed
